Removing the texture condition from the gate makes the existing fallback reachable; both renderers now return (191,191,191,191) for the null-texture cell, which is `Alpha = 0.75` against a white sample, the same number to the byte. The fix is deliberately scoped to alpha test: `DualTextureEffect` and `EnvironmentMapEffect` carry the same `!= nullptr` shape gate and are left to `WEBGPU-175`/`WEBGPU-176`, which own them. The null-texture row asserts no colour (what a missing sample yields is not something XNA pins down) — it asserts that `Always` draws, `Never` discards, and `Greater`/`Less` against the same alpha stay COMPLEMENTARY, i.e. the test is still evaluated against the effect's own `Alpha` rather than the draw being dropped or waved through. |
| WEBGPU-175 | `DualTextureEffect` coverage. | ✅ | **Gap:** 10 vs 2. Untested: the ×2 doubling of layer 0; a null `Texture`; a null `Texture2`; `Alpha`; `DiffuseColor` composition; the golden image. **EasyGL evidence:** `easygl_dualtextureeffect_{alpha,combined,doubling,golden,independent_uv,null_texture0,null_texture2}_test`. **Depends on:** `WEBGPU-159` for the independent-UV half. **Done 2026-09-05.** One shared parity fixture, `dual_texture_terms` (`WEBGPU-207`), **byte-identical between EasyGL and WebGPU, max diff 0**, 10/10 on each. FNA's `PSDualTexture` is `color.rgb *= 2; color *= overlay * pin.Diffuse`, so the fixture puts each of the four factors in its own column against a white second layer — the doubling, the overlay, `DiffuseColor` (1, 0.5, 0.25) so each channel scales differently, and `Alpha` — then composes all four in one cell that lands on (118,32,22), a value no dropped factor reaches. **Every texel is deliberately unsaturated**, which is the whole reason the fixture can see the doubling: `1 * 2` clamps straight back to `1`, which is how three CNA renderers once shipped without the `*2` at all (EasyGL's Task 383). **Negative control:** deleting `sample0.rgb * 2.0` from the WebGPU dual-texture WGSL turned 8 of the 10 checks red, every unsaturated cell landing on exactly half its expected value — and the two that stayed green were `both null` (`2 × 1 × 1` saturates to white either way) and the distinctness check, which is precisely the blindness the unsaturated texels exist to avoid. `Alpha` is measured under an **opaque** blend on purpose: XNA folds it into RGB on the CPU (`EffectHelpers.SetMaterialColor` sets `diffuse.rgb = DiffuseColor * Alpha`), so it must darken the pixel even where the alpha channel is discarded; 0.75 takes (200,180,120) to (150,135,90). **A second real WebGPU defect, the twin of `WEBGPU-174`'s, found and fixed.** `QueueDualTextureDraw` threw `std::invalid_argument: requires both texture0 and texture1 to be bound`, and `SelectStockVertexShapeEXT` additionally gated the family on both being non-null — so a `DualTextureEffect` missing either layer aborted the process where EasyGL renders. Opaque white is the identity for both factors in FNA's formula and is exactly what `EasyGLRenderer::EnsureDefaultWhiteTexture()` binds for a missing unit, so both sites now take the existing `pbrDefaultWhiteTexture_` fallback. **Negative control for that half:** restoring the refusal made the fixture abort again, so the three null cells really do traverse this route rather than passing by luck. The null `Texture` and null `Texture2` cells use **channel-reversed** texels ((100,90,60) against (60,90,100)) because the formula is symmetric in the two layers — with the same texel both cells produce the same pixel and a renderer that applied the fallback to the WRONG layer would pass. A third cell leaves BOTH unbound and must saturate to white, which is what pins the fallback as white rather than black or transparent. The row's "golden image" item is met in a stronger form than a per-renderer PNG: the two renderers' entire 128×64 frames match to the byte. The independent-UV half is `WEBGPU-159`'s, already closed, so this fixture holds both UV sets identical and varies only the factors. |
| WEBGPU-176 | `EnvironmentMapEffect` coverage. | ✅ | **Gap:** 12 vs 3. Proven on WebGPU: reflection face selection, Fresnel gating, `EnvironmentMapAmount=0`, emissive, fog. Untested: `EnvironmentMapSpecular`; several directional lights; a non-identity world transform's effect on the reflection vector; `EyePosition` changes; the Fresnel gradient across a curved surface; the golden image. **EasyGL evidence:** `easygl_environmentmapeffect_{specular,multilight,worldtransform,eyeposition,fresnel_gradient,combined,golden}_test`. **Done 2026-09-05.** One shared parity fixture, `env_map_terms` (`WEBGPU-207`), 4×4 cells, **14/14 on each renderer and the two frames agree within the parity tolerance (max diff 2, mean 0.04)**. **The cube is UNIFORM — the same colour on all six faces — and that is the design, not a shortcut.** `parity_compressed_cube` records why an environment fixture normally cannot be compared frame to frame: which face a reflection lands on depends on the pixel-centre convention, and EasyGL and WebGPU disagree about it (`WEBGPU-187`). A uniform cube deletes that variable — every direction returns the same texel — so everything left is exact arithmetic both renderers must reproduce. It costs nothing in coverage, because `EyePosition` and the world transform reach the pixel through the FRESNEL weight as well as through `reflect()`, and that weight is pure maths no sampling convention touches. **`EyePosition` is not settable** (XNA derives it as `Invert(View).Translation`), so each cell sets `View = CreateTranslation(-eye)` and `Projection = Invert(World * View)`, making `World*View*Projection` exactly the identity: the quad lands on the same pixels in every cell, so two cells differ in the shaded value alone and never in which pixels they cover. **Measured, each against its own twin:** `EnvironmentMapSpecular` adds exactly `EnvironmentMapSpecular * envmap.a` — (64,0,32) from (0.5,0,0.25) against a cube at alpha 128, on the two channels it names and neither other one, proving the add is scaled by the cube's ALPHA; `amount 0.5` is the exact MEAN of the amount-0 and amount-1 cells, computed from the frame rather than from a constant, which catches an ADD, a clamped weight or swapped lerp endpoints; at amount 1 the light count changes nothing, because the lerp keeps none of the lit half; the cube's alpha does not tint the lerped colour, only the specular add; three lights differ from one and are brighter. **A real WebGPU defect found and fixed: the Fresnel weight was computed per FRAGMENT, and unclamped.** XNA evaluates `ComputeFresnelFactor` in the VERTEX shader (`ComputeEnvMapVSOutput`) and Gouraud-interpolates the scalar, and D3D9 saturates it to [0,1] on the way out because it travels in `float4 Specular : COLOR1`. Recomputing per fragment from an interpolated normal is a different function once vertices carry different normals — `normalize` and `pow` are both non-linear — and it is invisible on a flat-normal quad, which is why every earlier WebGPU env-map test missed it. The fixture's last cell fans the four vertex normals outward: WebGPU read (83,97,55) where EasyGL read (77,124,72), and the whole-frame difference was **max 46**. Moving the term into `vs_main` with XNA's clamp took it to **max 2**, which is EasyGL's `precision mediump float` on an interpolated gradient. EasyGL records the same rule at its own `vFresnel` (`plans/plan_graphics.md` row 1112, clamp at SAMPLE-037), and OpenGL2, OpenGL4 and Magnum agree with it. **`webgpu_envmap3d_test.cpp` Check B had to be corrected with the fix, and this is worth stating plainly: it was asserting the defect.** Its derivation reasoned about the Fresnel term "at the quad's centre", which is the per-fragment model; under XNA's rule the quad's four VERTICES are what matter, and at corners (±1,±1,0.5) with the eye at the origin every one of them gives `dot(E,N) = 1/3` and a weight of exactly 2/3, so the centre is two thirds of the way to the face colour rather than black. The closed form predicts (0,170,170); the renderer now measures (5,167,165) — derived before it was run, not fitted to it. **A wider defect was found and deliberately NOT fixed here:** `Vulkan`, `SDL_GPU`, `LLGL` and `Sokol` all carry the same per-fragment, unclamped Fresnel in their own `env_map3d.frag.glsl`. Recorded as `plans/plan_graphics.md` row 1117 with the evidence and the verification recipe; none of them is this row's to change. |
| WEBGPU-177 | `SkinnedEffect` coverage. | ✅ | **Gap:** 14 vs 4. Proven: bone palette, `WeightsPerVertex` 1/2/4 in-browser, world normal, material, fog. Untested: identity-bone no-op; a pure translation bone; a two-bone blend at 50/50; `Vector4` blend indices as well as `Byte4`; specular; several lights; `VertexColorEnabled`; the golden image; the maximum bone count. **EasyGL evidence:** `easygl_skinnedeffect_{identity_bones,translation_bone,twobone_blend,vector4_bone_indices,specular,multilight,vertexcolor,weightspervertex,combined,golden}_test`, `skinned_effect_bones`. **Done 2026-09-05.** One shared parity fixture, `skinned_terms` (`WEBGPU-207`), 5×2 cells, **13/13 on each renderer and the two frames agree within the parity tolerance (max diff 2, mean 0.02, in the specular cell — EasyGL's `precision mediump float` on a `pow`)**. All nine untested items are covered. **The sharp cell is the 50/50 blend.** FNA's `Skin()` is `skinning += Bones[Indices[i]] * Weights[i]` — a weighted SUM of palette entries, not a selection — so blending identity with a translation of `2d` at half weight each must land the quad in exactly the same pixels as a single bone translating by `d`. The two cells are therefore compared to EACH OTHER rather than to a constant, and a renderer that picked the highest-weighted bone, normalized the weights, or summed only the first influence misses by a whole quad width. The palette cell uses index **71**, the last slot `SkinnedEffect::MaxBones` allows, and TRANSLATES — so a truncated or wrongly-indexed palette renders it unmoved, which is a different picture from the identity cell beside it. Each quad is a quarter of its cell wide and the translation moves it by exactly its own width onto a fixed probe strip, so "the quad moved" is read as two strips changing state rather than as an average drifting. **A real WebGPU defect found and fixed: a `Vector4`-declared `BLENDINDICES0` did not draw at all.** XNA's `VertexElementFormat` describes the BYTES in the buffer, not the register the semantic arrives in, so a content processor may write either format — the stock `SkinnedModelProcessor` writes `ConvertChannelContent<Vector4>("BlendIndices0")` and real XNA draws it (`plans/plan_fx.md` FX-127, EasyGL's own `easygl_skinnedeffect_vector4_bone_indices_test`). WebGPU's dispatch gated the skinned family on `stride == 52 || stride == 56`, so a stride-64 skinned vertex fell through to `DrawColoredPrimitives` and was refused as "not a stride-16 VertexPositionColor buffer" — a true statement about the wrong route. Two changes: the dispatch clause drops its stride test, because a skinned effect is a skinned draw whatever byte layout the declaration chose and `QueueSkinnedDraw` is the one place that knows whether a declaration can supply the five semantics; and `NormalizeSkinnedStreamEXT` rewrites any such declaration into the canonical stride-52/56 record at capture. **Why a rewrite rather than a second pipeline:** WebGPU has no vertex format that reads unsigned bytes as un-normalized floats — the trick EasyGL uses to serve both declarations from one shader — so an attribute-level fix means doubling all four skinned shaders. Bone indices are small integers (XNA caps the palette at 72), so a float index is exactly representable as the byte the canonical record wants, and everything downstream keeps seeing the one layout it already knows. The canonical strides keep their original memcpy path untouched. **Negative control:** replacing the float→byte conversion with a raw 4-byte `memcpy` of the float lane — the naive implementation — made the `Vector4` cell select bone 0 and sit still, turning exactly the two assertions that own that claim red and leaving the other eleven green. Verified afterwards: all five existing WebGPU skinned tests (`Skinned3D`, `SkinnedPbr3D`, `SkinnedEffect_{Fog,WorldNormal,Material}`) still pass, and nothing in the tree asserted the old refusal message. |
| WEBGPU-178 | `SpriteBatch` geometric and state behaviour corpus. | ✅ | **Gap:** 9 EasyGL programs vs 3 WebGPU ones (sort mode, render target, `ShaderEffect`). Untested on WebGPU: source rectangle (including one that runs past the texture, which XNA leaves unclamped so the sampler's address mode governs); rotation about a non-zero origin; non-uniform scale; sub-pixel/floating-point destinations; `SpriteEffects` flips combined with rotation; `layerDepth` ordering under each sort mode; the `Begin` transform matrix; blend-state leakage between batches; a destination inside a `RenderTarget2D` (target-local coordinates, `REMED-GFX-019`). **EasyGL evidence:** `easygl_spritebatch_{sourcerect,rotation,rotation_golden,scale,layerdepth,blendstate_leak,rendertarget_size}_test`, `sprite_effects`, `transform_matrix`. **Note:** WebGPU bakes sprite geometry on the CPU at enqueue time (`QueueSprite`), so these are cheap, fully deterministic pixel assertions. **Acceptance:** one WebGPU example covering the geometric family and one covering the state family, both with exact pixel oracles. **Done 2026-09-06.** Two shared parity fixtures as the row asks (`WEBGPU-207`): **`sprite_geometry`, 25/25 on each renderer**, and **`sprite_state`, 37/37 on each and byte-identical between them (max diff 0)**. **The texture is a four-quadrant colour chart**, not a flat fill: against a flat texture a source rectangle that read the whole image, a flip that did nothing and a rotation that turned the wrong way are all invisible, and every one of these cells would pass with the sampling completely wrong. **Measured:** a source rect running two texels PAST the texture is not clamped by XNA — the sampler governs, and `PointClamp` repeats the edge texel, so the sprite stays yellow instead of wrapping to red; a quarter turn takes the chart's red corner to the sprite's top-right whether the origin is the corner or the centre, which is what separates "the origin moved the sprite" from "the origin changed the rotation"; a non-uniform scale paints an 8×16 sprite carrying the whole chart squeezed, with the cell's right half clear; and both flips plus a half turn is the IDENTITY, which fails for any renderer that applies the flip after the rotation rather than to the texture coordinates. **The four sort modes are told apart by two overlapping pairs, not one.** `Deferred` and `Immediate` produce the same pixels by design, and — measured, after a first attempt got this wrong — reversing both the depth AND the draw order between two pairs keeps `Deferred` and `BackToFront` in lockstep so they cannot be separated at all. Pair 1 therefore draws the FRONT sprite first, which gives three distinct signatures for four modes, exactly as many as there are behaviours: `Deferred`/`Immediate` (blue, blue), `BackToFront` (blue, red), `FrontToBack` (red, blue). Each pair also carries two control probes on the sprites' non-overlapping parts, so an ordering verdict is never read off an empty or overdrawn cell. **A real WebGPU defect found and fixed, and it is the reason this row could not have been closed by inspection.** `GraphicsDevice.Viewport` came back **800×480 for a 128×96 backbuffer** — the XNA default size, never refreshed — while EasyGL reported 128×96. `SpriteBatch` derives its projection from that `Viewport`, so every sprite in the fixture drew at 128/800 of its size: twelve sprites correctly arranged inside an 18×16-pixel corner. Cause, measured with probes rather than inferred: `GraphicsDevice::RefreshViewport()` calls `renderer_->OnSurfaceChanged()` and then `GetViewportSize()` in the same breath, and WebGPU's `GetViewportSize()` answered from `physicalWidth_`, which only tracks the last `ConfigureSurface()` — the surface state already held the new 128×96 drawable while `physicalWidth_` still held 800×480. A one-frame program never gets the second chance a game loop would. `ComputeLogicalViewportForEXT(w, h)` now lets `GetViewportSize()` answer from the size the platform REPORTED; the draw-time path is unchanged, because by then the two agree. `parity_compressed_cube` had been hiding this: binding and unbinding a render target pushes an explicit viewport, which put its sprites back on the correct path. **The fixture now asserts the precondition directly** — the device's `Viewport` must cover the whole backbuffer — so the defect cannot return silently. **The one cell where the two renderers do NOT agree pixel-for-pixel is the half-pixel destination**, and that is `WEBGPU-187`'s subject rather than a tolerance to widen: exactly 32 pixels differ, all of them in that cell, and they are its top and left coverage edges — the two renderers round a fractional destination to a different pixel. Every other cell matches to the byte, and the fixture's assertions for that cell claim only what holds under either convention: the sprite's interior colour is unchanged. Verified afterwards: 255 `CnaTests` viewport/sprite/render-target cases pass, and all 31 WebGPU sprite, viewport, scissor, present and render-target tests pass. |
| WEBGPU-179 | Prove `SpriteFont` renders on WebGPU. | ✅ | **Gap:** 6 EasyGL programs vs 0. Glyph placement, spacing, newline handling, the default character and `SpriteEffects`/rotation/scale are shared `SpriteFont` logic and are covered renderer-neutrally by `SpriteFontTests`, so this is **not** six new tests. What is missing is any evidence that a `SpriteFont` string reaches WebGPU pixels at all. **Expected:** one WebGPU example that draws a known multi-glyph string with a newline and a default-character substitution and asserts glyph-cell pixels — enough to catch a renderer-level regression (wrong source rect, wrong row order, dropped batch flush). **EasyGL evidence:** `easygl_spritefont_{single_glyph,multiglyph_spacing,newline,default_char,effects_flip,effects_rotation_scale}_test`. **Done 2026-09-06.** One shared parity fixture, `sprite_font` (`WEBGPU-207`), **10/10 on each renderer and byte-identical between them (max diff 0)** — the row asked for a WebGPU example and this is that plus an EasyGL cross-check for the same source file. Deliberately narrow, as the row directs: the placement arithmetic is already `SpriteFontTests`' subject, and what is proven here is that it reaches pixels. **The atlas is three DIFFERENT colours, one per glyph, not three copies of white.** A white atlas lets a renderer sample the wrong glyph, or the same glyph three times, and still produce a picture that looks exactly right; with `A` red, `B` green and `C` blue, each drawn cell says which source rectangle it came from, and one wrong rect changes exactly one cell. Each glyph also carries a black marker in its own TOP-LEFT texel, which must arrive at the glyph cell's top-left pixel — the classic flipped-atlas row-order bug puts it at the bottom, and a solid glyph could not make that claim at all. The string is `"AB\nCX"`: the newline assertion checks BOTH halves of what a newline does (the pen moved down by `lineSpacing` AND back to the string's own x origin — a renderer that only moved down leaves line 2 indented, one that only reset x draws it over line 1), and `X` is absent from the font so the default character `A` renders in its place, in a colour neither neighbour has, with a further assertion that the substitution is not simply the previous glyph drawn twice. Two clear-colour probes keep it non-vacuous: the area above the string and the area past its last glyph are both untouched. |
| WEBGPU-180 | Device loss, step 1 — resolve the three questions the recovery inventory could not answer from source. | ✅ | **Supersedes the scope of `WEBGPU-107`**, which said only "implement `DebugSimulateContextLoss`". **Rewritten 2026-09-04, second pass (C9):** the audit's version of this row said "inventory what must survive", which was work the audit had already done — a placeholder audit task. That inventory is now written into this file as the *Device-loss recovery inventory* table below, and this row no longer produces it. **What is genuinely unresolved, and is this row's whole content — three questions, each answered with a probe in `spikes/webgpu-devicelost-spike/`, not by reading:** (1) **Object graph.** Can a lost `WGPUDevice` be replaced while the existing `WGPUInstance`, `WGPUAdapter` and `WGPUSurface` stay alive — i.e. does `wgpuSurfaceConfigure` accept a new device on an already-configured surface — or must the surface (and on Emscripten the canvas context) be recreated too? This decides whether recovery is renderer-internal or has to reach back into `CNA::Platform`. (2) **Event classification.** `WGPUDeviceLostCallbackInfo`/`WGPUDeviceLostCallback` deliver `WGPUDeviceLostReason_{Unknown,Destroyed,CallbackCancelled,FailedCreation}`; only some of those are a real driver-reported loss. Which reasons, on which callback mode and which thread, may raise `RendererDeviceEvent` through `GraphicsRendererCreateArgs::deviceEventCallback` — and what this pin actually delivers when the adapter is reset under it. (3) **Browser semantics.** Whether the Emscripten/emdawnwebgpu path can observe or simulate a loss at all, and therefore what `DebugSimulateContextLoss()` means there — a real destroy, a refusal, or a CPU-side flag. **Acceptance:** the three answers are committed in the spike's `README.md` and folded into `docs/webgpu-renderer.md`, leaving `181`/`182` as implementation against the table below. If a question turns out to be answerable from the pinned headers alone, record that and skip its probe — but record it. **Done 2026-09-06.** `spikes/webgpu-devicelost-spike/` (source + `README.md` with the full transcript), and the four facts folded into `docs/webgpu-renderer.md` under *Device loss: what the pin actually does*. **(1) ANSWERED — recovery is renderer-internal.** Measured: device 1 configures the surface and acquires; `wgpuDeviceDestroy`; a second device from the SAME adapter re-configures the SAME surface and acquires successfully. Instance, adapter and surface all survive a device replace, so nothing has to reach back into `CNA::Platform` or rebuild the window. **(2) ANSWERED, and the answer is "it never fires".** For an application-initiated destroy this pin delivers **no** `WGPUDeviceLostCallback` at all — not under `AllowProcessEvents` with `wgpuInstanceProcessEvents` pumped, not with `wgpuDevicePoll` on the device, not on releasing the last reference, and not under `AllowSpontaneous`. Four escalating attempts, zero callbacks. So `WEBGPU-182`'s debug simulate/restore path cannot be built on the callback: the renderer must set its own `deviceLost_` flag where it destroys the device and raise `RendererDeviceEvent` itself, and the driver-reported path stays unverified here for `WEBGPU-196`. The "which thread" half of the question is moot on native because nothing arrives. This is a statement about **v29.0.1.1**, not about WebGPU; the spike is the check when the pin moves. **(3) ANSWERED FROM SOURCE, and recorded as such rather than measured** — the row permits that provided it is said. `emdawnwebgpu`'s `library_webgpu.js` registers `device.lost.then(info => _emwgpuOnDeviceLostCompleted(...))` and its `emwgpuDeviceDestroy` is literally `device.destroy()`, which the WebGPU specification resolves with reason `"destroyed"`; `library_webgpu_enum_tables.js` maps only `{'undefined': 1, 'unknown': 1, 'destroyed': 2}`, so a browser can reach `Unknown` and `Destroyed` and nothing else. The web target therefore DOES get the callback the native pin withholds, and the two need different wiring. A browser confirmation has not been run and belongs with `WEBGPU-196`. **A FOURTH finding the questions did not ask for, and the one that constrains `WEBGPU-181`/`182` most:** `wgpuSurfaceGetCurrentTexture` on a surface whose device is lost does not return a failure status — it panics inside wgpu-native (`Parent device is lost`) and, because a Rust panic cannot unwind across the C ABI, **aborts the process**. There is no status to read and no exception to catch, so the probe that would have measured it is deliberately absent from the spike and its absence is the answer. `CanBeginDrawEXT()` returning false is what stands between a lost device and that abort: on this pin it is a safety mechanism, not a convenience for `Game`. |
| WEBGPU-181 | Device loss, step 2 — the framework-facing half: `CanBeginDrawEXT`, `SetContextRecoveryEnabled`, CPU shadow retention. | ✅ | Implement the parts that do not need a real device destroy: a `deviceLost_` flag that `CanBeginDrawEXT()` reports so `Game` keeps ticking `Update` without calling into an invalid device; `SetContextRecoveryEnabled()` so `Texture2D` knows whether it may free its CPU pixels (`gpuOnlyContent_`); `ShareCpuPixels()` on the texture renderers so a recreate has bytes to re-upload. **Depends on:** `WEBGPU-180`. **Tests:** a unit test that sets the flag and asserts `CanBeginDrawEXT()`/`Texture2D`'s retention behaviour changes accordingly. **Acceptance:** the contract's CPU-side half is real and tested without needing a lost device. **Done 2026-09-06.** `deviceLost_` + `CanBeginDrawEXT()`, `SetContextRecoveryEnabled()`, and `ShareCpuPixels()` on both `WebGPUTextureRenderer` and `WebGPUTextureCubeRenderer` (per face). `WebGPU_ContextRecovery`, **8/8**. **The claim with teeth is pointer identity, not content equality.** The renderer holds the SAME allocation `Texture2D::cpuPixels_` does — the test compares `GetCpuPixelsWeak().lock().get()` against `SharedCpuPixelsEXT().get()` — so the entire cost of context recovery on this renderer is one reference count and no copy is ever made. A test that compared CONTENTS could not tell a shared buffer from a duplicated one, which is exactly the mistake worth catching. **Negative control:** replacing the share with a `make_shared<vector>(*pixels)` copy turned exactly the two pointer-identity checks red and left the other six green. **`CanBeginDrawEXT()` is a safety mechanism here, not a convenience for `Game`** — `WEBGPU-180` measured that `wgpuSurfaceGetCurrentTexture` on a surface whose device is lost does not return a failure status but panics inside wgpu-native and aborts the process, so the flag is the only thing that can stop the acquire; the header says so where it is declared. `deviceLost_` is wired and read but nothing sets it yet: that is `WEBGPU-182`'s real destroy/recreate, and this row does not pretend otherwise. **One expectation of my own corrected mid-task, worth recording because it changes what the switch is FOR:** the test first asserted that with recovery off the framework keeps its own shadow while only the renderer drops its reference. It does not — `Texture2D::MaybeFreeCpuPixels()` frees `cpuPixels_` itself ("saving ~1x texture RAM"), which is the whole point of the switch, and the renderer holding nothing is a consequence of there being nothing to hold rather than a second, independent saving. **A deliberate limitation, documented rather than half-done:** `SetContextRecoveryEnabled(false)` is FORWARD-LOOKING, matching the interface's own wording ("future `Create*` calls will skip ... CPU shadow copies"); a texture that already shared its buffer keeps it, because releasing those needs a registry of live texture renderers that this renderer does not have and that `WEBGPU-182` will have to add anyway (a recreate must walk them to re-upload). The case the contract actually names — called at startup, before any texture exists — costs nothing either way. |
| WEBGPU-182 | Device loss, step 3 — real destroy+recreate, `DebugSimulateContextLoss`/`DebugRestoreContext`, and the driver-reported path. | ✅ | Implement the recreate itself and wire wgpu-native's device-lost callback to `GraphicsRendererCreateArgs::deviceEventCallback` so a genuine driver-reported loss raises `GraphicsDevice::DeviceLost`/`DeviceResetting`/`DeviceReset` (the `CnaDeviceEvent` channel, `plan_dx9.md` D9-34), which is a different direction from the debug hooks and must not be conflated with them. **Depends on:** `WEBGPU-181`. **Tests:** simulate loss, then assert that a texture uploaded before the loss still samples correctly, a render target still renders, a vertex buffer still draws and a bound render target is re-bound (`feedback: context-loss must rebind the active target`). **Environment note:** a *real*, driver-initiated device loss cannot be provoked reliably here; the simulated path can be, and the driver path may need external verification (`WEBGPU-196`). Do not mark ✅ on the simulated half alone without saying so in the row. `WEBGPU-107` closes when `180`–`182` do. **All four test cases are done, 2026-09-06, and what is NOT done is the driver-reported path, which the row's own environment note anticipates — this is the ✅ the row permits only if said, so it is said here.** `WebGPU_ContextRecovery` is **22/22**. **The device cycle:** `DebugSimulateContextLoss()` releases every registered resource's handles, then every device-owned object, unconfigures the surface and destroys the `WGPUDevice`, sets `deviceLost_` and raises `Lost`; `DebugRestoreContext()` raises `Resetting`, requests a NEW device from the surviving adapter, re-configures the SAME surface, rebuilds every registered resource, and raises `Reset`. `CanBeginDrawEXT()` goes false and back, each `GraphicsDevice` event fires exactly once, and the whole cycle raises **zero uncaptured validation errors**. The instance, adapter and surface are never rebuilt, which is `WEBGPU-180`'s measured finding put to work. **The two acceptance cases now proven:** a `Texture2D` uploaded BEFORE the loss still samples correctly afterwards — its content comes back from `WEBGPU-181`'s shared CPU pixels, or from the compressed block store for a BC texture — and a `VertexBuffer` created before the loss still draws. The second needed no rebuild at all, and finding out why is worth recording: this renderer's deferred replay builds its own vertex buffer from the CPU shadow at flush time and **nothing binds `WebGPUVertexBufferRenderer::buffer_` in a render pass**, so the native handle a loss kills was never on the draw path. Its `RecreateAfterDeviceLossEXT()` is deliberately empty, with that reason in the comment, rather than allocating GPU memory no draw reads. **The other two acceptance cases, added the same day:** a `RenderTarget2D` created BEFORE the loss still renders afterwards, and one that was BOUND when the device went is still the bound target after the restore — the clear and the sprite issued straight after the restore both land IN it, checked by sampling the target back onto the backbuffer rather than by reading a flag. Closing them was the bounded refactor the row predicted: the constructor now stores its four arguments (`depthFormat`, `mipMap`, `multiSampleCount`, `surfaceFormat`) and the native-object creation moved into `CreateNativeObjectsEXT()`, which the constructor and `RecreateAfterDeviceLossEXT()` share so the two cannot drift apart. **The target is recreated EMPTY, and that is XNA-correct rather than a shortcut** — a device reset discards a render target's contents, which is what `RenderTargetUsage` already names for a far cheaper event; what survives is the OBJECT (size, format, sample count, and its binding). **Two negative controls, each turning exactly its own claim red:** nulling `currentRenderTarget_` inside the simulated loss turned the two binding checks red and left the other twenty green; removing the render target's `RegisterDeviceResourceEXT` call — so it keeps handles from the destroyed device — made the first submit after the restore a wgpu-native `Validation Error` that panics and aborts the process, which is the failure the registration exists to prevent and the reason a live render target used to refuse the loss outright. **Still refused by name, and unchanged:** a `TextureCube`, `RenderTargetCube`, `Texture3D`, occlusion query or custom `ShaderEffect` cannot yet rebuild itself, so a live one is COUNTED and `DebugSimulateContextLoss()` throws `System::NotSupportedException` naming them while any exists. That converts what would be a dead native handle into this project's own refusal rule. **The mechanism is in place for all of it:** `IWebGPUDeviceResourceEXT` (release / recreate / detach) plus one registry on the renderer, with the destructor detaching every entry FIRST so a resource outliving the renderer never dereferences a destroyed owner from inside a destructor. Verified after the change: 136 of 138 WebGPU-labelled tests pass, the two failures being the pre-existing `DescriptorCapacityContract`/`PointSamplingContract` pixel-centre pair. **The driver-reported path stays unverified**, as the row's environment note anticipates: `WEBGPU-180` measured that this pin delivers no device-lost callback for an application-initiated destroy, so the flag is set by the debug hook and `OnDeviceLost` stays wired for a real loss the pin may one day deliver. |

#### Device-loss recovery inventory — settled here, so `WEBGPU-180` does not have to produce it

Written into this file on 2026-09-04 (second pass, C9) from the same source read the parity audit
did, so `WEBGPU-181` and `WEBGPU-182` are implementation against a contract rather than design
against a placeholder. Source-derived; not verified by running anything.

**The contract.** A WebGPU device loss must look to XNA code exactly like EasyGL's: `CanBeginDrawEXT()`
goes false so `Game` keeps ticking `Update` without calling into an invalid device; the renderer
raises `RendererDeviceEvent` through `GraphicsRendererCreateArgs::deviceEventCallback` so
`GraphicsDevice` fires `DeviceLost`/`DeviceResetting`/`DeviceReset`; and on recovery every resource
the app still holds is valid again with its contents, without the app re-uploading anything it did
not upload itself. Anything that cannot meet that must refuse by name, not degrade quietly.

| Live object | Rebuildable from data CNA already retains? | What recovery must do |
|---|---|---|
| `WGPUInstance` / `WGPUAdapter` / `WGPUSurface` | n/a — created from platform state | Answered by `WEBGPU-180` question (1): whether these survive a device replacement or must be recreated with it |
| `WGPUDevice` / `WGPUQueue` | yes, from `RequestAdapterAndDevice()`'s own parameters | Recreate, re-request the same feature set (`TextureCompressionBC` today; `Float32Filterable`/`Float32Blendable`/`TextureFormat16bitNorm` once `WEBGPU-199`–`201` land), re-run `PickSampleCount()` and reconfigure the surface |
| Swap-chain configuration, backbuffer depth texture, MSAA colour texture + view | yes | Recreate through the existing `ConfigureSurface()` / `RecreateMsaaColorTexture()` path at the current physical size and sample count |
| `Texture2D` storage | **yes for uncompressed** — `Texture2D::cpuPixels_` is the shared CPU shadow the framework already keeps, offered to renderers through `ITextureRenderer::ShareCpuPixels()` (WebGPU does not implement it today). **Yes for BC** — `compressedLevels_` is this renderer's own authoritative per-mip block store (`WebGPURenderer.hpp` 179-185) | Implement `ShareCpuPixels`, hold the shadow, re-create the texture and re-upload every defined level. `HasDefinedMipLevel()` already says which levels are real, so nothing is fabricated |
| `TextureCube` storage | yes, same two mechanisms — `ITextureCubeRenderer::ShareCpuPixels(face, pixels)` per face, plus the block store once `WEBGPU-206` lands | Re-create and re-upload per face and level |
| `Texture3D` storage | **no CPU shadow exists** | Either retain one on upload (the honest cost) or refuse recovery for `Texture3D` by name. Decide in `WEBGPU-181`; do not silently hand back an empty volume |
| `RenderTarget2D` / `RenderTargetCube` colour, depth, MSAA and per-face views | **contents are not retained anywhere, by design** | Re-create at the same width/height/format/depth format/sample count/level count and **clear**. XNA's own contract after a device reset is that render-target contents are gone; `RenderTargetUsage` decides only within a frame. State this rather than pretending to restore it |
| `VertexBuffer` / `IndexBuffer` | yes — buffer `GetData` is served from a shared CPU shadow above the renderer, so the bytes exist | Re-create and re-upload; preserve `SetDataOptions` semantics on the replay |
| The transient/deferred buffer pool and the SpriteBatch vertex ring | yes — pure scratch | Drop and re-allocate. Nothing in them survives a lost frame, and nothing should try to |
| The deferred command list (queued draws not yet replayed) | n/a | Discard. A loss between `Draw` and `Present` means that frame is gone; the `REMED-GFX-167` `WebGPUSampledResourceEXT` keep-alives must be released without touching the dead device |
| All twelve `WGPURenderPipeline` caches, bind-group layouts, pipeline layouts, shader modules | yes — sources are compiled-in WGSL | Full teardown and rebuild via `ClearAllPipelineCaches()`, which `WEBGPU-58` already established must recreate modules and layouts, not just pipeline objects. `WEBGPU-197`'s wider key changes the cardinality, not the mechanism |
| Samplers (16 slots) | yes — `SamplerState` is CPU state the framework holds | Re-create from the current per-slot state, including the `WEBGPU-160`/`161`/`205` additions |
| `WGPUQuerySet` (occlusion) | yes | Re-create; in-flight query results are lost, and `OcclusionQuery` must report not-complete rather than a stale value |
| Compiled-effect modules, once `WEBGPU-204` selects a route | yes, if the bytecode is retained | Retain the source bytecode and re-translate, as EasyGL does (`plan_fx.md` `FX-107`) |
| Bound state: current render target / MRT set, viewport, scissor, stencil reference, blend factor, per-slot samplers and textures | yes — all CPU-side | Re-apply after recreation, in the order `ConfigureSurface()` already establishes, before the first draw of the recovered frame |

**What this leaves open** is exactly the three questions in `WEBGPU-180`, and nothing else.


### Phase 70.2 — P2: coverage hardening and de-drift

| # | Task | Status | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| WEBGPU-183 | Establish WebGPU's `SurfaceFormat` refusal surface as a test, per resource kind. | ✅ | **Current behaviour (derived by reading, not by running):** `ClassifySurfaceFormatEXT` answers `Supported` for the **BC family only** (and only when the device enabled the BC feature); every other format returns `Defer`, and the framework rule it defers to (`Texture::ValidateFormat`) accepts only `SurfaceFormat::Color`. So on WebGPU a `Texture2D`/`TextureCube` is `Color` or BC and nothing else, a `RenderTarget2D` is `Color` only, and a `Texture3D` is `Color` only (that last one matches every renderer). Each refusal is a named exception, not a silent substitution. **EasyGL evidence:** `easygl_surface_format_throws_test` locks the equivalent surface down for EasyGL. **Expected:** the same lock-down for WebGPU, per resource kind, so `WEBGPU-184`'s additions are visible as a diff rather than as a behaviour change nobody notices. **Acceptance:** one test enumerating every `SurfaceFormat` × {Texture2D, TextureCube, Texture3D, RenderTarget2D, RenderTargetCube} and asserting accept-or-named-refusal. **Done 2026-09-06.** `SurfaceFormatRefusalSurfaceTests.cpp`, 26 formats × 5 resource kinds, **5/5 on WEBGPU and 5/5 on OPENGL33**. **The row's own "current behaviour" was already stale and the test records the real one:** `RenderTarget2D`/`RenderTargetCube` are no longer `Color` only — `WEBGPU-198`..`202` added `Single`, `Vector2`, `Vector4`, `HalfSingle`, `HalfVector2`, `HalfVector4` and `HdrBlendable`, and `WEBGPU-206` made the compressed cube real. Measured surface on WEBGPU: `Texture2D` and `TextureCube` accept `Color` + `Dxt1/3/5` + `Dxt5Srgb` + `Bc7`/`Bc7Srgb`; `Texture3D` accepts `Color`; both render-target kinds accept `Color` plus the seven float formats. `Rgba64` is absent because this adapter has no `TextureFormat16bitNorm` (`WEBGPU-201`). **There is deliberately NO per-renderer expectation list.** A list has to be rewritten every time a renderer gains a format — eight were added to WebGPU since this row was written — and a test that needs editing to stay true locks nothing down. What is invariant, and what is asserted, is the SHAPE: a resource either constructs and reports the format it was asked for, or refuses with `System::NotSupportedException` or `std::runtime_error`; never a silent substitution, never an unnamed exception. Run on both renderers it produces genuinely DIFFERENT surfaces and passes on both, which is the demonstration that it measures the renderer rather than a constant — and EasyGL's line makes `WEBGPU-184`'s gap concrete: its `Texture2D` accepts `Bgr565`, `Bgra5551`, `Bgra4444`, `NormalizedByte2` and `NormalizedByte4` where WebGPU refuses all five. **Negative control:** making `RenderTarget2D` construct its base as `Color` regardless of the requested format — XNA's own substituting behaviour, which CNA deliberately does not have — turned every non-`Color` accept red with the message naming `WEBGPU-163`. **One limitation stated rather than glossed:** `getFormatProperty()` echoes the constructor's argument, so this arm locks CNA's no-substitution rule but cannot see a format accepted at the API and stored as something else on the GPU. That needs a byte round-trip, which is `WEBGPU-184`'s own acceptance and which `WEBGPU-187` showed cannot be done through `Texture2D::GetData` at all (it reads the CPU shadow) — it has to go through a render target. |
| WEBGPU-184 | Store the packed 16-bit and normalized-byte `SurfaceFormat`s natively. | ✅ | **Missing behaviour:** `ClassifyWebGPUTextureFormat()` maps the six BC formats and returns `RGBA8Unorm` for everything else; `UpdatePixelsLevel()` unconditionally computes `levelW*levelH*4` bytes. `Bgr565`, `Bgra5551`, `Bgra4444`, `NormalizedByte2` and `NormalizedByte4` are consequently refused at construction. **EasyGL evidence:** REMED-GFX-244 — `EasyGLTextureRenderer::UploadLevel()` stores `Bgr565` as `GL_RGB565` directly and rotates `Bgra5551`/`Bgra4444` by one channel width into `GL_RGB5_A1`/`GL_RGBA4` (a pure bit permutation, no resampling), and both `NormalizedByte` formats use the ES3 signed-normalized set; `easygl_packed16_format_test`. These are `GraphicsProfile.Reach` formats a real XNA game may use. **Expected:** `WGPUTextureFormat_RGBA8Unorm` is wrong for all five; WebGPU has no 565/5551/4444 texture format at all, so the honest options are (a) keep refusing and say so in `GetAdditionalLimitationsTextEXT()`, or (b) expand to `RGBA8Unorm` at upload while preserving the exact XNA `SetData`/`GetData` byte round-trip through a CPU shadow. **Decide and record which**, then implement it; `NormalizedByte2`/`4` map cleanly to `RG8Snorm`/`RGBA8Snorm` and should be done regardless. **Tests:** exact `SetData`→`GetData` round-trip per format plus a GPU sampling assertion. **Depends on:** `WEBGPU-183`. **Done 2026-09-06.** **THE DECISION: (a) for the three packed 16-bit formats, native support for the two normalized-byte ones.** Recorded where a caller reads it — `GetAdditionalLimitationsTextEXT()` now carries the clause and its reason. `Bgr565`/`Bgra5551`/`Bgra4444` stay refused by name because WebGPU has no 16-bit packed colour texture format at all, so option (b) would mean a texture reporting one format while the GPU holds another — the shape of the defect `WEBGPU-163` was, and the one `WEBGPU-183`'s surface test exists to prevent. Choosing (b) would have traded a named refusal for a silent lie one row after building the test that forbids it. `NormalizedByte2`/`4` are now `rg8snorm`/`rgba8snorm`, stored natively with no conversion. **`NormalizedByteFormatTests.cpp`, renderer-neutral, 4/4 on WEBGPU and 4/4 on OPENGL33**, plus the surface test recording the change as the diff the row asked for: WEBGPU's `Texture2D` line gained both formats. **Three defects had to be fixed before the format worked, each measured rather than predicted:** (1) `RGBA8Snorm` is not RENDERABLE in core WebGPU, and the texture descriptor asked for `RenderAttachment` unconditionally — "Texture usages TextureUsages(RENDER_ATTACHMENT) are not allowed on a texture of type Rgba8Snorm", after which every view and every write on that texture failed too. The flag is now conditional. (2) That in turn means `GenerateMips2D`'s render-pass blit has nowhere to run, so a MIP-MAPPED NormalizedByte texture is refused by name rather than handed back with undefined levels; a game supplying its own levels is not what that rejects. (3) `UpdatePixels` rejected a correct upload with "stride is too small", because it compared against a hardcoded `width * 4` while the shared layer passes `levelW * getBytesPerTexel()` — 2 for NormalizedByte2. Sizing the upload, the readback and the construction-time check from the format fixed it. **The sampling assertion is the one that matters, and the negative control proves why.** Storing the two formats as UNSIGNED instead — the classifier's old default arm — leaves all three round-trip tests GREEN, because `Texture2D::GetData` for a plain texture reads the framework's CPU shadow and never asks the GPU (`WEBGPU-187`'s finding). Only the sampling test goes red, on all three of its assertions: a texel of (-1,-1,-1) must reach the shader as -1 and clamp to black, +1 must reach it as white, and the two must be far apart — where an unsigned store puts them two units apart at 0x81 and 0x7F. `UnsupportedFormatConstructionTest`'s signed-normalized list gained `WebGPU` in the same change, so the surface stays locked. |
| WEBGPU-185 | Partial-rectangle and partial-box texture transfers, and mip-level transfers. | ✅ | **Gap:** EasyGL has `texture2d_partial_rect`, `texture2d_mip`, `texturecube_partial_rect`, `texturecube_mip`, `texturecube_faces`, `texture3d_partial_box`, `texture3d_partial_box_readback`, `texture3d_mip`, `texture3d_slices`; WebGPU has whole-level `GetData` tests only. The renderer code accepts sub-rectangles, so this is verification, not implementation — but `Texture2D::SetData` takes a **read-modify-write** path through `renderer_->GetData()` for a partial level-0 update when the CPU shadow is gone, and nothing exercises it on WebGPU. **Acceptance:** non-origin, non-full sub-regions on 2D, each cube face, and a 3D sub-box, written and read back exactly, at level 0 and at a level > 0. **Done 2026-09-06.** `PartialTextureTransferTests.cpp`, renderer-neutral, **4/4 on WEBGPU and 4/4 on OPENGL33**: a non-origin 4×3 rectangle at level 0 of an 8×6 texture, the same at MIP LEVEL 1 (where the offset is measured against the level's own size, not the base's), a 3×2 rectangle on cube face 2 with all six faces checked, and a 3×3×2 sub-BOX of a 6×5×4 volume. Every test checks the SURROUNDINGS as carefully as the target, because the failure this shape has is a write that landed at the origin or rewrote the whole level — in which case the region itself looks right and everything around it is wrong. Backgrounds and patches both encode their own coordinates, so a write at the wrong offset from a flat source cannot hide. **The read-modify-write half could not be tested, and finding out why is this row's real result.** That route is reachable only through a `RenderTarget2D` — for a plain `Texture2D` a partial update with no shadow THROWS by design — and writing that test showed that **`RenderTarget2D::SetData` is silently discarded, on EasyGL and WebGPU alike, for a partial rectangle AND for a whole level, with no exception.** `IRenderTargetRenderer` derives from `ITextureRenderer`, whose `UpdatePixels`/`UpdatePixelsLevel` have EMPTY default bodies, and neither renderer's render-target class overrides them — so the shared layer reads the target back, merges the caller's rectangle into it exactly as `REMED-GFX-127` intends, and hands the result to a no-op. Measured on both: after `SetData`, the target still reads back what was RENDERED into it. Recorded as `plans/plan_graphics.md` **row 1118** with the mechanism, the measurement and two acceptable fixes; not fixed here, because it is shared CNA behaviour and not this row's to widen into. The file steers around it with a comment naming the row, the way `parity_basic_effect_vertex_color` does for row 1116, so nothing asserts the broken behaviour as correct. The READBACK half of the route does work and is covered by `NpotTexture.*RenderTarget*`. |
| WEBGPU-186 | Sampler filter / address-mode / anisotropy pixel coverage across slots. | ✅ | **Gap:** EasyGL has 8 programs (`texture_address_mode`×3, `texture_filter_linear_golden`, `texture_filter_point_vs_linear`, `texture_mip_filter_effect`, `texture_anisotropic_effect`, `texture2d_anisotropic_singlelevel`); WebGPU's address-mode coverage lives inside `WebGPU_Clear_Readback` and there is none for filters, mip filters or anisotropy. Note that only slots 0 and 1 currently reach a stock draw (`slotSamplers_[0]`/`[1]`), which is right for the stock effects but should be asserted rather than assumed. **Acceptance:** point vs linear magnification differ in the expected texels; each mip filter differs; anisotropy on a steeply-angled quad differs from trilinear; a single-level texture under `Anisotropic` does not misbehave. **Done 2026-09-06.** One shared parity fixture, `sampler_filters` (`WEBGPU-207`), 4×3 cells, **12/12 on each renderer**. **Its oracle is INTERNAL, not a frame diff, and that is the design.** `WebGPU_PointSamplingContract` records that the two renderers do not yet agree about XNA's pixel-centre convention (`WEBGPU-187`), and MAGNIFICATION filtering is exactly where a half-texel disagreement shows — the same `Linear` filter lands its gradient half a texel apart. Measured: the two frames differ by up to 255 (mean 15.2), which is correct rather than a tolerance to widen. Every claim is therefore an A/B within one renderer, true on both. **How point and linear are told apart without naming a pixel:** a 4×4 checkerboard magnified 8× is, under `Point`, exactly two colours — every pixel is one of the two texel values — and under `Linear` a gradient whose in-between pixels take values neither texel has. The discriminator is the COUNT of such intermediate pixels, which no pixel-centre convention changes: **0 of 676 under Point and 672 of 676 under Linear, identically on both renderers.** `MinPointMagLinearMipPoint` magnifies linearly and produces 672 as well, which is what separates a renderer reading the whole filter enum from one distinguishing only Point from everything else. **A correction to my own first assertion, worth recording because it is a trap:** that cell was first compared with the Point cell by AVERAGE, and both average (125,125,120) exactly — a checkerboard's mean is the same blurred or not. The claim had to become per-pixel. **Mip chain:** a 4×4 texture drawn at 2×2 samples level 1, whose colour appears nowhere in level 0, so "which level did the sampler reach" is a colour question; both mip filters reach it. **Anisotropy:** a foreshortened ground plane, 64×64 fine checkerboard with generated mips, drawn twice — `Linear` against `Anisotropic` at MaxAnisotropy 16 — differs by 156 per channel on EasyGL and 46 on WebGPU, both far above the threshold, and the assertion is made only when the device reports the capability. The first attempt reused the 4×4 board and its flat synthetic level 1 and differed by ONE unit, correctly: a sampler resolving detail that is not there resolves nothing. **Negative control:** dropping `MaxAnisotropy` to 1 collapses WebGPU's difference from 46 to 1 and turns exactly that assertion red, so the property really does reach the sampler. A single-level texture under `Anisotropic` renders the board rather than black, and a sampler set on **slot 1** leaves a `SpriteBatch` draw pixel-identical — the slot claim the row asked to be asserted rather than assumed. |
| WEBGPU-187 | NPOT texture behaviour. | ✅ | **Gap:** `easygl_npot_texture_test` has no WebGPU counterpart. WebGPU has no POT restriction, so this is a cheap confirmation that odd sizes upload, mip, sample and read back correctly — including the `GetData` row-alignment path, where a 256-byte `bytesPerRow` alignment makes NPOT widths the interesting case. **Done 2026-09-06.** `modules/graphics/tests/.../NpotTextureTests.cpp`, renderer-neutral, **10/10 on WEBGPU and 10/10 on OPENGL33**. Sizes 13×7, 17×11, 1×5, 64×3, 3×3 and 1×1: exact round-trips, a sub-rectangle at offset (3,2) that divides nothing, and XNA's flooring mip rule (13×7 walks 13×7, 6×3, 3×1, 1×1). Every texel encodes its own coordinates, so a readback that returned the right NUMBER of texels from the wrong OFFSETS fails on content rather than on size. **Writing it found that the obvious reading of this row is wrong, and the row would have been closed by a test that measured nothing.** `Texture2D::GetData` for a plain texture never calls the renderer at all: it is served from the framework's own CPU shadow (`gpuOnlyContent_` is false for a plain texture, and with the shadow freed it throws rather than reading the GPU). So the `Texture2D` half of this file cannot reach the staging alignment the row names — **measured, not assumed:** breaking WebGPU's texture readback stride left all seven of those cases green. The renderer's readback is reachable only through a **RenderTarget2D**, which is `gpuOnlyContent_` and therefore asks the renderer, so three render-target cases were added and they are the ones that own the alignment claim. **Negative control on the half that matters:** replacing the render-target readback's padded `bytesPerRow` with a tight `levelWidth * 4` stride failed `AnOddWidthRenderTargetReadsBackThroughTheRenderer` at texel (0,1) — the first texel of the second row, exactly where a tight stride first lands wrong — while `AnAlreadyAlignedRenderTargetReadsBackThroughTheRenderer` (64 wide, whose row is exactly 256 bytes and needs no stripping) stayed green. That pairing is why both widths are in the file: it separates "it works" from "it works for the reason we think". The 1×5 case is the extreme, a 4-byte row against a 256-byte alignment. |
| WEBGPU-188 | Buffer and draw-route validation coverage. | ✅ | **Gap:** EasyGL has 17 programs here; WebGPU has 3 plus the instanced pair. Missing on WebGPU: 32-bit index draws end to end; `DrawUserPrimitives`/`DrawUserIndexedPrimitives` (both reach the ordinary `Ex` routes through shared temporary buffers, so this is verification); `startIndex`/`baseVertex`/`vertexStart` ranges that run off the end being rejected before native submission; `PrimitiveType` validation including `PointListEXT`; every `VertexElementFormat` the declaration may name; a draw with no index buffer and a draw with no vertex buffer; `BufferUsage.WriteOnly` `GetData` refusal; dynamic-buffer churn. **EasyGL evidence:** `easygl_{draw_range_validation,draw_user_primitives_custom,draw_user_indexed_primitives_32,draw_noindexbuffer,draw_novertexbuffer,primitivetype_validation,vertex_formats,buffer_usage,vertexbuffer_setdata,vertexbuffer_indexbuffer_getdata,dynamic_buffer_stress}_test`. **Done 2026-09-06.** `DrawRouteValidationTests.cpp`, renderer-neutral, **10/10 on WEBGPU and 10/10 on OPENGL33**, covering every item on the list: a 32-bit index buffer that round-trips and draws; both user-primitive routes; four out-of-range indexed arguments and two non-indexed ones; a draw with no vertex buffer and an indexed draw with no index buffer; all five `PrimitiveType`s including `PointListEXT`; all twelve `VertexElementFormat`s; `BufferUsage.WriteOnly`'s readback refusal; and 24 `SetData`-then-draw cycles on one buffer. **Every rejection test is PAIRED with the same call at a legal argument**, and that pairing is the row's real content: "the draw threw" proves nothing on its own, because a renderer that refused everything would satisfy a refusal-only test completely. The legal call is made before the refusals and again after them, so a refusal that left the device unusable is caught too. The enum sweeps use the accept-or-named-refusal shape `WEBGPU-183` established: a `PrimitiveType` or a `VertexElementFormat` is either drawn or refused with `System::NotSupportedException`, never accepted and read as something else, and a refusal carrying any other exception type fails the test by name. |
| WEBGPU-189 | Render-target semantics coverage: usage, MSAA change, reported properties. | ✅ | **Gap:** `RenderTargetUsage.PreserveContents` vs `DiscardContents` is threaded (`REMED-GFX-136`) and never tested on WebGPU; `ApplyMultiSampleCount()` clears every pipeline cache and is never tested for a mid-run MSAA change; `RenderTarget2D`/`RenderTargetCube` public properties (`DepthStencilFormat`, `MultiSampleCount`, `RenderTargetUsage`, `LevelCount`) are never asserted. **EasyGL evidence:** `easygl_{render_target_usage,msaa_change,rendertarget2d_properties,rendertargetcube_properties,rendertargetcube_depthformat,rendertargetcube_sample,rt_roundtrip,gfx164_bound_msaa_alpha}_test`. **Depends on:** `WEBGPU-164`/`165` for the mip and per-instance-MSAA rows. **Done 2026-09-06.** `RenderTargetSemanticsTests.cpp`, renderer-neutral, **6/6 on WEBGPU and 6/6 on OPENGL33**. **`RenderTargetUsage` is asserted as a PAIR, and the pair is what makes it a test.** `PreserveContents` must still hold what was drawn when the target is bound a second time, and `DiscardContents` must not — the two targets are painted, unbound and re-bound identically and differ only in the usage they were created with. The two assertions are logical complements on the same operation, so no renderer can satisfy both without honouring the flag: one that never discards fails the second, one that always discards fails the first. Asserting only the first, which is the obvious test to write, passes on a renderer that ignores the flag entirely. **The MSAA change is about what changing it DOES, not about pixels:** `ApplyMultiSampleCount()` clears every pipeline cache, so the draw after a change is the first user of an empty one and a pipeline keyed against the previous sample count either fails validation or lands in the wrong attachment shape. A 4-sample target and a 0-sample target are painted and read back in one run, in that order, and then the multisampled one again — so the transition is exercised in both directions rather than once. Each reads back exactly the colour it was cleared to. **A convention corrected mid-task:** a target created with `preferredMultiSampleCount = 0` reports **0** on WebGPU, not 1 — XNA spells "not multisampled" as 0 while a GPU spells it as 1, and both mean the same thing. Every sample-count claim here is therefore made about the EFFECTIVE count, so neither spelling is asserted as the right one. The property tests assert the values the caller PASSED, in the one direction a renderer may reduce them (`applied <= requested`, and a depth request may widen to a format the renderer has but may never come back as `None`). A sixth test paints all six `RenderTargetCube` faces different colours and reads each back: a renderer sharing one face view writes every face with the last colour, and all six would read as face 5. |
| WEBGPU-190 | Render-state pixel coverage: blend, depth-stencil, rasterizer, viewport. | ✅ | **Gap:** WebGPU's native maps are complete and correct (13 blend factors, 5 blend functions, 8 compare functions, 8 stencil operations, separate colour/alpha, `BlendFactor`, `MultiSampleMask`, `ColorWriteChannels`, two-sided stencil, depth bias) but almost none of it is pixel-verified; EasyGL has 22 programs across these four families. **Expected:** four WebGPU examples — one per state object — sweeping the enum values with a deterministic oracle, in the style of `easygl_depthstencilstate_stencil_ops_test` and `easygl_blendstate_separate_functions_test`. Include the two edge cases EasyGL calls out: state leakage between draws/batches, and a viewport sub-region combined with a render-target switch. **Done 2026-09-06.** Four shared parity fixtures (`WEBGPU-207`), one per family, all green on both renderers: **`blend_states` 10/10 and byte-identical**, **`depth_states` 9/9 and byte-identical**, **`stencil_states` 11/11 and byte-identical**, **`rasterizer_viewport` 9/9**. **Blending is an equation, not a golden image.** Every cell's expected colour is COMPUTED from source, destination and state — `Opaque`, `Additive`, premultiplied `AlphaBlend`, `NonPremultiplied`, `Modulate` (`DestinationColor`/`Zero`), `ReverseSubtract`, separate colour/alpha, `BlendFactor` and a red-only `ColorWriteChannels` — and the source (200,60,30) at alpha 128 against destination (40,90,220) is chosen so `SourceColor` and `SourceAlpha`, `DestinationColor` and `InverseSourceColor`, and every other near-miss land on different numbers. Nine expected colours, mostly pairwise distinct, asserted as a non-vacuity precondition. **Depth is an 8×3 signature matrix**, the same shape `WEBGPU-174` needed and for the same reason: one depth relationship cannot separate eight functions. Each is run against a fragment nearer, equal and farther, giving pairwise-distinct triples (`DDD`, `...`, `D..`, `DD.`, `.D.`, `.DD`, `..D`, `D.D`), all eight matching on both renderers, with the table asserted balanced at 12 draws of 24. **Stencil asks the BUFFER what the operation produced** rather than inferring from a colour: clear to 0x10, apply the operation with reference 0x30, then gate a second quad on `Equal` against the value the operation should have written. All ten expectations are computed from the operation's definition. Two extra cells drive `Decrement` and `DecrementSaturation` from ZERO, which is the only place the wrapping and saturating forms differ at all — 255 against 0. **Culling is asserted as a PAIR of windings in one draw call**: a cell that only checked "the triangle vanished" cannot tell culling from a triangle never submitted, a degenerate winding, or a renderer that culls everything; checking that the OTHER winding survived rules out all three. Both row-190 edge cases are covered: a cull mode set and then replaced does not leak into the next draw, and a viewport survives being re-set after a render-target round trip. **Two measurements worth keeping.** `RasterizerState.DepthBias` is NOT a fraction of the depth range — at −0.01 the biased quad loses the tie on both renderers and at −1.0 it wins on both, so the unit is the smallest resolvable depth difference (`glPolygonOffset`'s `units` and its equivalents) and a smaller bias does nothing at all. The cell therefore asserts the DIRECTION a negative bias moves a surface, which is the renderer-independent part. And the probes for the winding cells had to become 5×5 squares inside each triangle's right-angle corner: a probe spanning the half-cell averages triangle and background together and reads as neither. **`rasterizer_viewport` is the one fixture of the four whose frames are not byte-identical**, and the difference is exactly localized: 276 pixels of 12800 (2.2 %), every one of them in the four cells that draw TRIANGLES, none in the quad cells. That is the diagonal hypotenuse rasterized under the two pixel-centre conventions — `WEBGPU-187`'s subject — and not a tolerance to widen. |
| WEBGPU-191 | Resource lifetime and disposal stress. | 🟨 | **Gap:** 8 EasyGL programs vs `WebGPU_DisposedGuard` alone. WebGPU's deferred-replay architecture makes this *more* dangerous than EasyGL's, not less: a `Texture2D` disposed between `Draw` and `Present` is exactly what `REMED-GFX-167`'s `WebGPUSampledResourceEXT` keep-alive exists for, and nothing stresses it. **Expected:** dispose a texture/buffer/render target while it is queued in a deferred draw; dispose while bound as the current render target; double-dispose; destroy resources out of order relative to the renderer; a create/dispose churn loop under a sanitizer. **EasyGL evidence:** `easygl_{bound_resource_dispose,device_dispose_order,disposed_resource,double_dispose,handle_release,move_semantics,resource_events,resource_leak}_test`. **Acceptance:** no use-after-free under ASan and no wgpu-native validation error in any case. **Partly done 2026-09-06 — the validation half is closed, the ASan half is not.** `WebGPU_ResourceLifetimeStress`, **10/10 with zero uncaptured errors**: a `Texture2D` disposed between its queued sprite draw and the flush; a `VertexBuffer` disposed between its queued draw and the flush; a `RenderTarget2D` disposed immediately after being unbound; a double `Dispose()` followed by further device use; and 32 create-draw-dispose cycles inside one frame. **The oracle is the renderer's own uncaptured-error count, not "it did not crash".** wgpu-native reports a use-after-free of a view, buffer or attachment as a validation error, and a test checking only for a crash passes on a renderer quietly submitting invalid work — which is what a keep-alive bug actually does, since the freed handle usually still points at memory nothing has reused. Every case also checks the FRAME still contains what it should, so "no errors" cannot be satisfied by dropping the work. **Negative control, and it is emphatic:** clearing `command.texture.keepAlive` in `QueueSprite` — that is, removing `REMED-GFX-167` — makes the very first case abort the process before it prints anything: `Error in wgpuQueueSubmit: Validation Error — In a set_bind_group command, BindGroup with 'CNA WebGPU SpriteBatch BindGroup' label is invalid`, then a Rust panic across the C ABI. So the keep-alive is load-bearing and this test is what stands on it. (The failure arrives as a process abort rather than a printed FAIL, which is still a non-zero exit and still caught, but worth knowing when reading a future failure.) **Why 🟨 and not ✅:** the row's acceptance names AddressSanitizer, and that half is not done. It needs a sanitizer build of this configuration — a multi-hundred-megabyte build directory of its own, which the project's build rules ask to be justified rather than created by default. The recipe is recorded in the test's own header (`cmake -S . -B cmake-build-webgpu-asan -DCNA_GRAPHICS_RENDERER=WEBGPU -DCMAKE_BUILD_TYPE=Debug -DCMAKE_CXX_FLAGS=-fsanitize=address`, then this same binary). The uncaptured-error oracle catches the same CLASS of bug here, because wgpu-native validates a handle before dereferencing it, but that is an argument and not a measurement, and the row stays partial until someone runs it. |
| WEBGPU-192 | Presentation and device-lifecycle coverage. | 🟨 | **Gap:** WebGPU has `WebGPU_PresentMode` only. Missing: backbuffer resize (see `WEBGPU-162`, which needs this test anyway); `PresentationParameters` round-tripping; `GraphicsDevice.Reset()` raising `DeviceResetting`/`DeviceReset` and the state that survives; `PresentInterval`/VSync; a real window resize as opposed to a programmatic one. **EasyGL evidence:** `easygl_{backbuffer_resize,real_window_resize,presentation_parameters,present_interval,graphicsdevicemanager_vsync,device_reset_events,device_validation}_test`. **Four of the five done 2026-09-06; the fifth is named below.** `PresentationLifecycleTests.cpp`, renderer-neutral, **5/5 on WEBGPU and 5/5 on OPENGL33**: every `PresentationParameters` field round-trips (including `Bounds`, which is derived and must follow rather than lag); all four `PresentInterval` values round-trip; `Reset()` raises `DeviceResetting` then `DeviceReset`, **each exactly once and in that order**, with `DeviceLost` not raised; a reset applies the new backbuffer size AND leaves the device usable; and two resets in one run both apply. **The reset is asserted through its events AND through what survives it, because the two say different things** — a reset that fired its events and left the device at the old size passes an event-only test, one that resized silently passes a size-only test, and neither is a correct reset. The size half also checks the VIEWPORT followed the new backbuffer, which is the regression guard for `WEBGPU-178`'s stale-`Viewport` defect, and the two-reset case is what catches a renderer that rebuilds its swap chain correctly once but caches something size-derived. **What is NOT done, precisely:** a REAL window resize, as opposed to the programmatic `Reset()` path. The reference test (`easygl_real_window_resize_test`) provokes it by calling `SDL_SetWindowSize` directly, which is a native call this project's platform boundary reserves for the sanctioned edges, and the boundary-clean alternative — `CNA::Platform::IPlatformWindow::SetSize()` — is reachable only through `GraphicsDevice::GetPlatformWindowInternal()`, which is private. So closing this needs a deliberate decision about which of the two routes a renderer example may take, and that decision is worth making on its own rather than in passing. Everything else the row lists is covered. |
| WEBGPU-193 | A shared, deterministic EasyGL↔WebGPU parity corpus. | ✅ | **Gap:** `WEBGPU-123` compares exactly one diagnostic triangle across four renderers. That was enough to settle "is WebGPU's rasterization sane"; it is nowhere near enough to claim renderer equivalence. **Expected:** a small set of renderer-neutral pixel fixtures — not a port of 200 EasyGL binaries — run under both renderers and compared programmatically: SpriteBatch (source rect + rotation + flip + tint), the four blend presets, the sampler modes, a depth-sorted pair, a stencil gate, both cull modes, wireframe, a render-target round trip, one draw per stock effect, three vertex declarations of the same mesh, a multi-stream draw, and an instanced draw. Reuse the `scripts/run-webgpu-parity-test.sh` harness. **Tolerance:** compare interior pixels, or use a justified tolerance where rasterization fill rules legitimately differ (`WEBGPU-123` measured EasyGL diverging from three other renderers on 57 triangle-EDGE pixels alone) — never declare a whole feature unequal on an edge rule. **Depends on:** most of Phase 70.0 and 70.1. **Acceptance:** the corpus runs in CI-shaped form and its per-fixture verdicts are recorded in `docs/webgpu-renderer.md`. **Rescoped 2026-09-04, second pass (C8):** the comparison *mechanism* is now `WEBGPU-207` and is built before Wave 1; this row is the corpus-complete milestone that runs it over the finished behaviour, and it inherits `207`'s fixture convention and tolerance rule rather than defining its own. **Done 2026-09-06.** **29 fixtures, every one passing its own assertions under BOTH renderers, 23 byte-identical**, run end to end by the new `scripts/run-parity-corpus.sh`, and the per-fixture verdict table recorded in `docs/webgpu-renderer.md` as the acceptance asks. Every item on the row's list is covered: SpriteBatch source rect/rotation/flip/tint (`sprite_geometry`), the four blend presets and the rest of the family (`blend_states`), sampler modes (`sampler_filters`, `sampler_max_mip_level`, `sampler_lod_bias`), a depth-sorted pair (`depth_states`, and `sprite_state`'s layerDepth matrix), a stencil gate (`stencil_states`), both cull modes (`rasterizer_viewport`), wireframe (`fill_mode_wireframe`), a render-target round trip (`render_target_mip`, `hdr_render_target`, `sprite_state`), one draw per stock effect (`basic_effect_*`, `alpha_test_*`, `dual_texture_terms`, `env_map_terms`, `skinned_terms`), the same mesh under four vertex declarations (`vertex_semantics`), a multi-stream draw (`multi_stream_split`) — and the one entry nothing covered, an **instanced draw**, added here as `instanced_draw`. **The corpus runner's real content is its POLICY table, not its loop.** Each fixture is `strict` (identical at tolerance 2), `allow:N` (at most N pixels may differ, with the measurement and the reason) or `internal` (frames not compared, with the reason a comparison is inapplicable), and the policy lives in the script where a reviewer sees it. A new fixture is `strict` by default and joins the exception table only with a measurement and a sentence — which is the mechanism the row's tolerance rule asks for, made enforceable rather than advisory. **`fill_mode_wireframe` was the one fixture the strict default caught**, at 367 of 32768 pixels; measured, all of them are the drawn LINE pixels, where GL's line rules and a WebGPU line list rasterize a diagonal differently. **Every one of the six exceptions has the same single cause** — the XNA pixel-centre convention `WEBGPU-187` owns — and each is confined to coverage edges rather than to a feature the renderers disagree about. **The instanced fixture found a real limit of the shared stock path, recorded in its header:** a per-INSTANCE `COLOR0` does not reach the stock vertex-colour shading at all (measured on EasyGL: every instance shades black, with both a `Vector4` and a packed `Color` element), so the fixture varies placement rather than colour and puts three instances in a six-cell grid — the three cells that must stay CLEAR are what a fixture drawing an instance everywhere could not check. |
| WEBGPU-194 | De-drift the WebGPU documentation and the stale in-source comments. | ✅ | **Confirmed drift (2026-09-04, planning-only run — nothing was edited):** (a) `docs/webgpu-renderer.md`'s *Important limitations* list is **backwards** — it lists `RenderTargetCube` `mipMap=true` as throwing and per-face cube MSAA as ignored, both of which `WEBGPU-114` implemented, while omitting `CreateRenderTarget2D(mipMap=true)`, which really does throw; (b) the same list still says `TextureCube` mip regeneration is open, though `WebGPUTextureCubeRenderer::SetData` triggers `GenerateMipsCubeFace`; (c) `docs/webgpu-vs-vulkan-deviations.md`'s "Wireframe: refused, not emulated" states a rationale this audit disproved (`WEBGPU-153`); (d) `docs/sampler-state-support.md` says nothing about WebGPU's `AddressW`/`MaxMipLevel`/LOD-bias behaviour (`WEBGPU-160`/`161`); (e) `plan_fx.md` §10.3 files WebGPU under "unsupported by design" on a premise the pinned package contradicts (`WEBGPU-166`). **Expected:** each corrected in the commit of the row that changes the behaviour, and whatever is left over swept here. **Acceptance:** no document states a limitation the renderer does not have, or omits one it does. **Done 2026-09-06.** **Four of the five were already corrected by the rows that changed the behaviour, exactly as the row intended, and this audit's job was to verify that rather than to assume it.** (a) `docs/webgpu-renderer.md` now says per-face MSAA and `mipMap=true` cube regeneration are implemented (`WEBGPU-114`), and the `CreateRenderTarget2D(mipMap=true)` throw is recorded as removed by `WEBGPU-164` — verified against the source, where the throw's site now carries the comment "mipMap=true used to throw here". (b) Cube mip regeneration is described as implemented in the same section. (c) `docs/webgpu-vs-vulkan-deviations.md`'s heading is now *"Wireframe: emulated by edge expansion, not by a polygon mode"* and reports the capability as **true**. (d) `docs/sampler-state-support.md` carries WebGPU rows for `MaxMipLevel` (`WEBGPU-161`), `AddressW` (`WEBGPU-160`) and the LOD bias (`WEBGPU-205`), including the section naming the shader-emulation boundary. **(e) was still wrong, and is corrected here.** `plan_fx.md` §10.3 said WebGPU "consumes WGSL", reconsidered "only if a SPIR-V route into wgpu-native is proven". A SPIR-V route is in the pinned headers: `WGPUShaderSourceSPIRV` and the instance feature `WGPUInstanceFeatureName_ShaderSourceSPIRV` are declared by **both** `wgpu-native v29.0.1.1` and the browser's `emdawnwebgpu` port, with `wgpuHasInstanceFeature()` to ask at runtime. What is NOT established is whether either implementation advertises it — that is a runtime question nobody has measured, and SPIR-V is not in the WebGPU specification, so a browser answering yes would be surprising. The v1 scope verdict is unchanged; only its stated premise is, and `WEBGPU-166`/`203`/`204` own the measurement. **The sweep the row's second half asks for:** a new *What the 2026-09-06 coverage batch changed* section in `docs/webgpu-renderer.md` covering the capabilities this document OMITTED — a null `Texture`/`Texture2` now renders instead of aborting (`WEBGPU-174`/`175`); `EnvironmentMapEffect`'s Fresnel is per-vertex and clamped (`176`); `SkinnedEffect` accepts any declaration supplying its five semantics, not only stride 52/56 (`177`); `NormalizedByte2`/`4` are stored natively while the packed 16-bit three stay refused, with the reason (`184`); the MSAA capability answers from the probe (`195`); `CanBeginDrawEXT()` is a safety gate rather than a convenience (`180`/`181`); and the stale-`Viewport` fix (`178`). It also records the three fixtures whose frames are deliberately NOT byte-identical and the single cause they share, so a future reader does not "fix" them by widening a tolerance. |
| WEBGPU-195 | Report `MultiSampleAntiAliasing` from the device probe rather than the permissive default. | ✅ | **Current behaviour:** the capability falls through to `IGraphicsRenderer`'s `true`. WebGPU already probes the device empirically (`PickSampleCount()` creates a scratch texture inside a `WGPUErrorFilter_Validation` scope), so it *knows* whether any count above 1 is available; it simply does not answer with it. **EasyGL evidence:** `EasyGLRenderer::SupportsCapability(MultiSampleAntiAliasing)` reads `GL_MAX_SAMPLES` and returns false on the ES2 generation. **Expected:** answer from the probe, cached. Low severity — on this machine the adapter supports 4× — but it is the same truthfulness rule `WEBGPU-115`/`134`/`135` established. **Done 2026-09-06.** `SupportsCapability(MultiSampleAntiAliasing)` now returns `Supports4xMsaa()`, the same cached probe `PickSampleCount()` acts on, so the declaration and its consumer are one answer rather than two. `Supports4xMsaa()` became `const` with a `mutable` cache, because probing the device is a question about it rather than a change to the renderer. **The row is right that the VALUE does not move on this machine, which is exactly why the test does not assert one.** It asserts that the declaration and the consumer AGREE — a claim that holds on any adapter and fails on precisely the divergence the row names. Two arms are shared across every renderer (a false capability may not hand back a multisampled target; a multisampled target may not come with a false capability), and a third, stronger arm is asserted **only under `CNA_RENDERER_IS(WebGPU)`**: a TRUE capability must produce a multisampled target. That one is renderer-scoped on purpose — here both answers come from one probe and cannot legitimately disagree, whereas renderers still reporting the shared permissive default while applying 1 sample are a different matter and are not dragged into this row. **Negative control:** restoring the permissive `return true` while forcing the probe to report no MSAA turned the test red on exactly its `WEBGPU-195` arm, with the message naming the row. Verified green on WEBGPU and on OPENGL33 (where the strong arm is inert), plus 26 MSAA/multisample/capability cases on WEBGPU. |

| # | Task | Status | Notes |
